In fact, during a recent stakeholder meeting where I couldn't go back and re-run my models, I ended up completely freezing due to sheer worry and panic.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you've been experiencing this intense need to check and re-check your work analyses, and has it been getting progressively worse over time?\n\nPatient: This really started about two years ago after a major workplace restructuring that significantly increased my responsibilities. Yet, despite knowing it's unreasonable, I still feel this overwhelming, intense urge to keep checking just to achieve absolute certainty. It's incredibly frustrating because my logical brain knows it's too much, but I just can't seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned recognizing your checking behaviors as excessive, but still feeling compelled to do them. Can you tell me if there are any specific situations or triggers that make you more aware of the irrationality of these behaviors, or if there's anything that helps you temporarily gain control over them?"

Honestly, nothing seems to help me gain control over these urges right now, especially since my usual coping routines no longer work.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these intrusive 'what-if' thoughts and urges to check in response to specific external stimuli, or are they more internally driven, arising from your own thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: It's really a mix of both. While the urge is triggered by an external event like finishing a dataset or analysis at work, the real driving force is highly internal. It comes from my own persistent \"what-if\" worries about making a hidden mistake and this overwhelming need to feel absolutely certain before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that you recognize your checking behaviors as excessive, but still feel compelled to do them. Can you tell me if you've ever had a period where you didn't experience these urges or 'what-if' thoughts, or if there was a time when you felt more in control of them?\n\nPatient: Yes, before the organizational restructuring two years ago, I felt much more in control and managed my career for decades using structured habits like detailed project plans and version control, which colleagues admired as thoroughness. Back then, I wasn't driven by uncontrollable anxiety, but once my responsibilities and the public scrutiny increased, the 'what-if' thoughts and compulsive checking intensified to this unmanageable level.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors that are not related to your work or the fear of making mistakes in your analyses, such as fear of contamination, symmetry, or harm to others?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced any of those other types of fears or behaviors, like contamination or symmetry. My focus is entirely on my work and this overwhelming fear of making analytical mistakes in my datasets. It really is just limited to my data and analysis projects."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see us today?\n\nPatient: I’m stuck double- and triple-checking my analyses for hours, even when I know they’re done, and it’s starting to derail my projects. I just can't seem to stop myself from doing it, and it's becoming a real problem at work.\n\nDoctor: Do you also experience repetitive behaviors or mental acts in other areas of your life, such as excessive cleaning, ordering, or counting, beyond just your work analyses?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed those kinds of behaviors like excessive cleaning, ordering, or counting. My struggle is almost entirely focused on checking and re-checking my work analyses. Outside of my data projects, things are pretty normal.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what specifically triggers your need to repeatedly check your analyses, is it the fear of making a mistake or something else?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's absolutely the fear of making a mistake. My brain latches onto these \"what-if\" thoughts, like making a tiny error in a dataset that leads to the city making a bad policy decision. These worst-case scenarios replay in my head, and I feel this intense need to be absolutely certain I didn't miss some hidden mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do these 'what-if' thoughts and the fear of making a mistake also intrude into your mind when you're not actively working on your analyses, or are they primarily present when you're directly engaged with your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, they absolutely intrude even when I'm not working, and I just can't shut off the replay of those worst-case scenarios in my mind. These thoughts follow me everywhere, making it so hard to focus that I've ended up staying late at the office, skipping my salsa nights, and turning down hikes. The anxiety and need for certainty are just constantly running in the background, even during my personal time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience significant anxiety or distress when you're unable to perform your checking behaviors, such as when you're in a situation where you cannot re-check your analyses?\n\nPatient: Yes, the anxiety is absolutely paralyzing when I can't check, because my mind gets flooded with thoughts of making a catastrophic error that ruins city policy. In fact, during a recent stakeholder meeting where I couldn't go back and re-run my models, I ended up completely freezing due to sheer worry and panic.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how long you've been experiencing this intense need to check and re-check your work analyses, and has it been getting progressively worse over time?\n\nPatient: This really started about two years ago after a major workplace restructuring that significantly increased my responsibilities. It has gotten progressively worse over the past year, and now I'm missing deadlines, getting called into meetings about my productivity, and skipping my usual hikes and salsa nights because I'm staying so late to re-check everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repetitive checking behaviors and the 'what-if' thoughts are entirely reasonable and justified, or do you recognize them as excessive or unreasonable, even if you feel compelled to do them?\n\nPatient: I absolutely recognize that they are excessive and don't make sense, especially since I usually know deep down that the numbers are correct. Yet, despite knowing it's unreasonable, I still feel this overwhelming, intense urge to keep checking just to achieve absolute certainty. It's incredibly frustrating because my logical brain knows it's too much, but I just can't seem to stop myself.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned recognizing your checking behaviors as excessive, but still feeling compelled to do them. Can you tell me if there are any specific situations or triggers that make you more aware of the irrationality of these behaviors, or if there's anything that helps you temporarily gain control over them?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what specific situations make me more aware of the irrationality, since the frustration of knowing it's illogical is just always there. Honestly, nothing seems to help me gain control over these urges right now, especially since my usual coping routines no longer work.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these intrusive 'what-if' thoughts and urges to check in response to specific external stimuli, or are they more internally driven, arising from your own thoughts and worries?\n\nPatient: It's really a mix of both. While the urge is triggered by an external event like finishing a dataset or analysis at work, the real driving force is highly internal. It comes from my own persistent \"what-if\" worries about making a hidden mistake and this overwhelming need to feel absolutely certain before I can move on.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that you recognize your checking behaviors as excessive, but still feel compelled to do them. Can you tell me if you've ever had a period where you didn't experience these urges or 'what-if' thoughts, or if there was a time when you felt more in control of them?\n\nPatient: Yes, before the organizational restructuring two years ago, I felt much more in control and managed my career for decades using structured habits like detailed project plans and version control, which colleagues admired as thoroughness. Back then, I wasn't driven by uncontrollable anxiety, but once my responsibilities and the public scrutiny increased, the 'what-if' thoughts and compulsive checking intensified to this unmanageable level.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced any obsessive thoughts or compulsive behaviors that are not related to your work or the fear of making mistakes in your analyses, such as fear of contamination, symmetry, or harm to others?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced any of those other types of fears or behaviors, like contamination or symmetry. My focus is entirely on my work and this overwhelming fear of making analytical mistakes in my datasets. It really is just limited to my data and analysis projects.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your obsessive thoughts are focused on making mistakes in your analyses, but can you confirm if you've ever had any brief periods where you felt the urge to perform other types of compulsive behaviors, such as arranging things in a specific order or feeling an intense need for symmetry, even if it wasn't related to your work?\n\nPatient: No, I have never experienced those specific urges, like needing symmetry or arranging things in a particular order. My obsessive thoughts and checking behaviors are strictly focused on my data analyses and the fear of making a work-related mistake. It really has never expanded beyond my worries about my datasets.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe if your obsessive thoughts about making mistakes in your analyses are typically triggered by specific internal cues, such as a specific thought or image, or are they more broadly related to the context of your work and the potential consequences of errors?"
  }

I end up obsessing over the fear that the city will make a bad policy decision because of my mistake.
